- [x] AD-315 Secondary / text darkened to #051c39 — operator: "#051c39
      eta add koro eta ektu dark eta 2nd coolor ke replace korbe",
      following "#070b14 replace this color by this color #092d50" and
      the supplied pair primary `#0b5ed7` / secondary `#092d50`.

      Both `--color-text` and `--color-secondary` held `#092d50`, so both
      moved. Contrast measured **before** applying, not after — every
      pair improves: body text on white 13.97 -> 17.05, on the page bg
      13.00 -> 15.87, white on a dark panel 13.97 -> 17.05, primary-soft
      text on a panel 12.19 -> 14.88.

      `--color-primary-bright` (#0a6cff) is still load-bearing and should
      not be collapsed into `primary`: on the darker ground plain
      `primary` measures **2.92:1**, under the 3:1 floor for graphical
      elements, while `primary-bright` reaches 3.74:1. The comments in
      `app/globals.css` quoting the old ratios were corrected in the same
      edit.

      Verified on the running site: resolved custom properties read
      `--color-text: #051c39` and `--color-secondary: #051c39`, seven
      elements paint `rgb(5, 28, 57)` and **zero** still paint the old
      `rgb(9, 45, 80)`; no `092d50` remains in any `.ts` / `.tsx` / `.css`
      source.

      **Open for the operator to decide:** `--color-text` and
      `--color-secondary` are now the same value again. They are separate
      tokens for a reason — one is type, one is a surface — and keeping
      them coupled means a future tweak to either drags the other along.
      Worth re-splitting (a slightly different panel ground from the body
      text) or deliberately aliasing one to the other.

- [x] AD-316 Dynamic Popup and Custom Alert redesigned, with entrance and
      exit motion — operator: "dynamic popup , Custom Alerts ei duita
      feature ke aro shundor and professional kore daw, jeta frontend e
      show korbe,, jokhon ashbe shundor kore ekta soft animation or
      transition dey jeno."

      Both storefront widgets (`components/storefront/dynamic-popup.tsx`,
      `site-alert.tsx`) were unstyled-by-token and appeared instantly.
      Colours moved onto `@theme` tokens (`bg-white` / `text-neutral-*`
      and the hard-coded `#111` are gone; the operator-chosen background
      and button colours are still honoured inline, since those are
      configured content, not chrome).

      **Motion.** Both mount only while visible, so entrances have to be
      keyframes — a transition has no previous value to animate from.
      Exits flip a `data-exiting` attribute to the reverse keyframes and
      the component waits for `animationend` before unmounting, so the
      frame is never cut. New CSS in `app/globals.css`: `th-popup-in/out`,
      `th-scrim-in/out`, `th-alert-in/out`, `th-alert-timer`. The corner
      alert's four positions share one keyframe pair via a
      `--th-alert-from` custom property, so each corner animates away from
      its own screen edge.

      **The popup is now a native `<dialog>` + `showModal()`** rather than
      a hand-rolled fixed overlay. That buys the focus trap, Escape, the
      inert background and top-layer stacking — the previous version
      claimed `aria-modal="true"` while providing none of them. Added on
      top: body scroll lock (not covered by `<dialog>`), backdrop-click
      dismissal, and focus moved to the card so the popup does not open
      with a focus ring sitting on the X.

      **The alert's auto-close now pauses on hover and on focus**, with a
      countdown bar that pauses in lockstep. Pause is implemented by
      re-running the timer effect: the cleanup banks the remaining time
      and the next run starts a fresh timer for exactly that remainder,
      so the bar never disagrees with the time actually left.

      **Four defects found by measuring rather than eyeballing:**
      * The dialog rendered pinned top-left. Two causes stacked:
        Tailwind's preflight zeroes every margin, killing the UA's
        `margin: auto` centring, and the UA's `width: fit-content` beats
        `inset: 0`, so the box hugged its card. Fixed with explicit
        `width/height: 100%` plus flex centring, guarded on `[open]` —
        an unguarded `display: flex` would override
        `dialog:not([open]) { display: none }` and pin the popup on
        screen permanently.
      * **Escape closed the dialog outright and stranded the scroll
        lock.** Chrome routes Escape through a CloseWatcher, and
        cancelling that requires sticky user activation — a visitor who
        presses Escape before clicking anything gets `preventDefault()`
        on `cancel` silently ignored. `display: none` then landed before
        the exit animation could run, `animationend` never fired, and
        `document.body.style.overflow` stayed `hidden` with the popup
        invisible. Now intercepted at `keydown` (no activation condition)
        with `onClose` as a safety net that lands the same end state.
      * The close chip on the popup image measured **2.89:1** over a
        worst-case white photo, under the 3:1 floor for graphical
        elements. `bg-text/45` -> `/60` puts it at 4.57:1.
      * Under `prefers-reduced-motion` the global rule collapses the
        countdown bar to ~0ms while the JS timer still runs its full
        duration — a progress bar that lies. The bar is hidden in that
        mode; the alert still auto-closes on time.

      `sessionStorage` in the alert moved from an effect to
      `useSyncExternalStore`. It is client-only, so a lazy `useState`
      initialiser breaks hydration and an effect means a synchronous
      setState on mount (which `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect` rejects).
      One consequence worth keeping: the dismissed flag is what `shown`
      derives from, so it is written *after* the exit animation, not when
      the close is requested — writing it early unmounts the alert
      mid-animation. The popup is the opposite case and writes it early
      on purpose: a click on its CTA navigates away and would otherwise
      beat the animation to the finish.

      `eslint` clean (`tsc` skipped — dev live). **Verified against the
      live widgets on the running site** (a real popup and a real alert
      are enabled in the database, so this was real data, not a fixture):
      popup centred to the pixel (card centre 720,500 in a 1440x1000
      viewport), `showModal` in the top layer, focus inside the dialog
      and on the card, scroll locked while open and released after; exit
      via Escape, backdrop click and the X each play `th-popup-out` and
      then unmount; alert enters with `th-alert-in` and the correct
      per-corner offset, survived 7.6s of hover against a 5s auto-close
      and closed after the pointer left; at 400px wide both keep a 16px
      gutter with zero horizontal overflow; under emulated reduced motion
      the bar is hidden, the alert still closes on schedule and Escape
      still releases the scroll lock.

      **Not verified with live data:** the popup's CTA button branch —
      the enabled popup has no button text configured. The markup the
      component emits was injected into the live card and checked
      visually; the branch itself is structurally unchanged from the
      previous version.

- [x] AD-317 Security audit of the whole site — operator: "website er
      security audit daw , ekta md file daw.."

      Report: **`SECURITY_AUDIT.md`** at the project root — the
      operator asked for it outside `docs/`, which also matches the
      existing `ADMIN_FUNCTIONALITY_AUDIT.md` at root. Supersedes the
      narrower `docs/SECURITY_AUDIT_P17.md` (2026-09-05), which scoped
      out response headers, payments, and everything built since.
      (A copy was first written to `docs/SECURITY_AUDIT_2026-09.md`;
      the root file is canonical.)

      **0 critical, 1 high, 6 medium, 10 low.** The application-layer
      model is sound — no anonymous path to another user's data was
      found. The weaknesses cluster in the HTTP response layer and in
      controls that assume a trustworthy staff member.

      **Confirmed with direct evidence, not just code reading:**
      * **F-01 (high) — zero security response headers.** Live `curl -D`
        on `/` returns only Vary/Link/Cache-Control/Content-Type. No CSP,
        HSTS, X-Frame-Options, nosniff, Referrer-Policy or
        Permissions-Policy, on the storefront *or* `/admin` — so the
        admin panel is framable and clickjackable.
      * **F-03 (medium) — open redirect.** `safeReturnPath` rejects
        `//evil.com` and `://` but not a backslash. Verified in Node's
        URL parser (the same WHATWG parser browsers use): `/\evil.com`
        and `/\/evil.com` both pass the guard and resolve to
        `https://evil.com/`. The OAuth callback is *not* affected — it
        re-prefixes with `publicOrigin()` and absolute parsing collapses
        the backslash back to a same-origin path.
      * **F-02 (medium) — uploaded SVGs are an XSS primitive.** Live
        fetch of a real upload returns `Content-Type: image/svg+xml`
        with no Content-Disposition and (per F-01) no nosniff or CSP, so
        a scripted SVG executes in the site's own origin. Needs
        `media.upload`, so it is a staff-compromise amplifier rather
        than an anonymous hole.

      Other mediums: spoofable `X-Forwarded-For` defeats every IP rate
      limit (the per-email buckets still hold, so spraying is the real
      exposure, and audit-log IPs are untrustworthy); no staff MFA or
      lockout; `GATEWAY_SECRETS_KEY` derived by a single unsalted SHA-256
      with a 16-char minimum (fast offline brute force of merchant
      credentials if the DB leaks); password policy is length-8-only.

      **Verified sound, recorded so it is not re-litigated:** all **193**
      exported admin server actions carry a permission guard *and* a
      same-origin check (the only unguarded exports are staff
      login/logout, correctly); no IDOR in any customer-scoped module;
      every `$queryRaw` is a tagged template with no `*Unsafe`/`Prisma.raw`
      anywhere; all six `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` sinks traced to a
      sanitizer or a hex validator; payment confirmation validates
      server-side against the gateway API and binds amount to both
      `payment.amount` and `order.totalAmount`, rejects duplicate refs,
      and locks the row `FOR UPDATE`; B2B KYC docs live outside
      `public/`; Argon2id with OWASP parameters and no user enumeration;
      `npm audit` clean (0 across the full tree); zero `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`
      vars and no sensitive logging.

      **Found while auditing:** `npm run test:security` is **currently
      red** (1/16 failing) — its blanket `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` ban
      now fires on six known-good sinks added since P17. All six are
      safe, so this is not exploitable, but a permanently-red gate stops
      catching the next genuinely unsafe sink. Logged as F-08 with the
      fix (path allowlist carrying a reason per entry).
      `npm run test:payments` passes 47/47.

      No code was changed by this task — audit only, per the operator's
      request for a report.

- [x] AD-318 Security remediation Phase 1 — inventory integrity (DSA-01,
      DSA-02). Operator-driven phased remediation of the deep audit.

      **DSA-01.** `ProductStock.productId` is unique (one stock row per
      product) while `CartItem` is unique per
      (cart, product, variant, colour) — so one product in two colours is
      two cart lines sharing one stock row. Availability compared every
      line against a single pre-loop snapshot, and `reserved` was written
      as an absolute value per line, so the second write discarded the
      first. Deterministic, no race needed. Fixed with a `claimedByStock`
      accumulator and one `{ increment }` per stock row.

      **DSA-02.** Nothing in the codebase ever decremented `reserved`.
      New `lib/orders/stock-reservation.ts` with release / convert /
      re-reserve, each claiming `Order.stockState` atomically via
      `updateMany({ where: { id, stockState } })` so a double release is
      structurally impossible — release is reachable from both the order
      path and the payment path. Stock writes are single clamped SQL
      statements, never read-modify-write.

      Migration `20260912211917_order_stock_state`: enum + column +
      `CHECK (reserved >= 0 AND reserved <= quantity)`. Verified 0
      existing violations before adding it. One row repaired
      (`p-ram-ddr4` reserved 5 -> 0) after a dry run; drift now 0.

- [x] AD-319 Security remediation Phase 2 — refund money safety (DSA-04,
      DSA-07).

      **DSA-04.** `completeRefund` reached the gateway through unlocked
      read-then-check logic, so two concurrent completions both paid out
      real money. `payoutRef @unique` never helped: it is consulted only
      after the money has moved, and two gateway calls return two
      different refs. Fixed with a single conditional write claiming
      `payoutStatus PENDING -> PROCESSING`; only that caller reaches the
      gateway. Deliberately NOT wrapped in a transaction spanning the
      gateway call — a row lock held across a 15s HTTP timeout would
      block the refund queue.

      **DSA-07.** The "already in progress" guard was an unlocked
      read-then-create. Now inside a transaction holding
      `SELECT … FROM "Order" … FOR UPDATE`, with partial unique index
      `Refund_one_open_per_order` as the structural backstop (verified 0
      existing duplicates). P2002 is caught and mapped to the same
      friendly message.

      Migration `20260913050831_refund_payout_claim`.

      **Residual risk, deliberately left:** `gatewayFetch` reports a
      timeout and a refusal identically, so a payout that succeeded but
      whose response was lost is retried. SSLCommerz is safe — it gets
      `refund_trans_id = refund.code` as an idempotency key. bKash is not
      known to dedupe this way; that one case still warrants checking the
      gateway before retrying. Documented in the code.

      **Method note for both phases:** every regression test was
      validated by reverting the fix and confirming the test fails.
      That caught a false pass in the first inventory test, which
      asserted only "order rejected" and was passing on a shipping
      fixture error — it now asserts the rejection reason.

      Suites: `test:inventory` 21, `test:payments` 53 (was 47),
      `test:security` 1/16 failing (pre-existing, DSA-08).
      `tsc --noEmit`: 5 errors, all pre-existing in untouched files, so
      `npm run build` compiles but cannot pass type-check. Flagged, not
      fixed — outside these phases' scope.

- [x] AD-320 Security remediation Phase 3 — admin privilege containment
      (DSA-03).

      Three permissions were each silently equivalent to full Admin,
      because authorization answered "may you perform this operation?"
      but never "how much privilege may this operation hand out?":
      `staff.add` (create an Admin account), `staff.edit` (promote
      yourself — only self-*disable* was blocked), `roles.manage` (grant
      your own role all 194 permissions, or strip the Admin role).

      New `lib/auth/privilege-containment.ts` enforces the standard
      delegated-administration rule: **you cannot grant access you do not
      hold yourself.** Permissions are read fresh from the database, not
      taken from the caller, so a stale session cannot widen what it may
      delegate. Because the Admin role holds every permission, the rule
      is invisible to a real Admin — asserted by a test, not assumed.

      Also enforced: `Role.isSystem` (present in the schema and seeded on
      `role-admin` with a comment saying it must not be editable, but
      never read as a guard until now), a self-role lock, and a
      last-administrator guard so the panel cannot be left with nobody
      holding `roles.manage`. Live data made that last one real: there is
      exactly **one** Admin account.

      No migration. No schema change.

      **Test-design lesson worth keeping.** The negative validation —
      reverting the fix to prove the tests catch it — ran the escalations
      for real and *actually stripped the seeded Admin role to 1 of 194
      permissions and left the only administrator with no role at all*
      (fixture cleanup deleted the weak role, and Staff.roleId is
      SetNull). Repaired immediately, but the root cause was the suite,
      not the guards: **a suite that exercises guards over shared seed
      state must snapshot and restore that state in `finally`,
      unconditionally.** `check-privilege-containment.ts` now does, and
      was re-validated by breaking every guard again — 9/11 failed and
      the database self-healed with no manual repair.

      Suites: `test:privilege` 11 (new), `test:payments` 53,
      `test:inventory` 21, `test:security` 1/16 pre-existing failure.
      `tsc --noEmit`: the same 5 pre-existing errors, none in touched
      files.

- [x] AD-321 Security remediation Phase 4 — wallet consistency (DSA-06),
      plus the pre-existing type errors blocking the production build.

      **DSA-06.** `adjustCustomerWallet` read the balance, added the delta
      in JavaScript and wrote the absolute result. `$transaction` gives
      atomicity but not isolation: under READ COMMITTED two concurrent
      adjustments read the same balance and wrote the same total. Now a
      single guarded atomic statement —
      `updateMany({ where: { id, walletAmount: { gte: -amount } },
      data: { walletAmount: { increment: amount } } })` — which puts the
      zero floor inside the same write as the adjustment. `balanceAfter`
      is read back inside the transaction, where our own UPDATE holds the
      row lock, so the ledger can never record a total the row does not
      hold. Migration `20260913061500_wallet_non_negative` adds
      `CHECK (walletAmount >= 0)` (verified 0 negative rows first).

      Reverting the fix proved how bad it was: **five concurrent debits
      of 500 all succeeded against a balance of 1000**, and five
      concurrent credits left balance 1500 against a ledger of 2500.
      Note the CHECK constraint alone would NOT have caught it — lost
      updates left a positive but wrong value. The atomic guard is the
      real fix; the constraint is only the floor.

      **Second defect found by the new test, not by reading.** The first
      run died with P2028 transaction timeouts. Cause: `writeAuditLog`
      uses the pooled global client, not `tx`, and was called from inside
      the interactive transaction — so each of five concurrent
      adjustments held one of the pool's `max: 5` connections and then
      waited for a sixth. A pool deadlock that surfaced as a timeout,
      nothing wallet-shaped. Audit logging is non-blocking by design, so
      it now runs after the commit. **Worth checking other
      `writeAuditLog` calls that sit inside a `$transaction`.**

      **Build now passes for the first time.** The 5 long-standing type
      errors are fixed: `MediaFolder` gained `"promotions"` (the
      promotion banner uploader already passed it) plus its `folderLabel`
      case; two `formatMoney({ amount, currency })` call sites dropped
      the excess `currency` (the helper only takes `{ amount }`);
      `isNewArrival` added to two ProductSummary fixtures.
      `npx tsc --noEmit` = **0 errors**, `npm run build` = **exit 0**.

      **Known, pre-existing, NOT fixed:** `npx eslint .` reports 13
      errors across 6 admin components (`react/no-unescaped-entities`,
      `set-state-in-effect`). Zero overlap with any file touched in
      phases 1-4. So `npm run lint` does not pass repo-wide.

      **Suite quirk worth knowing:** `test:payments` reports 53 with the
      dev server running and 51 without — two checks fetch the live app
      and skip silently when it is down. A skipped check is
      indistinguishable from a passing one in that output.

      Suites: `test:wallet` 11 (new), `test:privilege` 11,
      `test:inventory` 21, `test:payments` 53,
      `test:security` 1/16 pre-existing failure.

- [x] AD-322 Security remediation Phase 5 — public order tracking
      (DSA-05).

      `/track/{orderNumber}` needed no session, and order numbers are a
      dense sequence since AD-305, so the page was an oracle over the
      whole order table: walk the numbers, read exact order volume and
      growth, harvest every courier `trackingCode`. Two individually
      reasonable decisions — short numeric order ids, and guest tracking
      — combined into the leak.

      **Chosen design: order number + last 4 digits of the phone.** No
      schema change, and links already sent to customers still resolve —
      they land on a verify step instead of breaking. A random public
      token would be stronger but would invalidate every tracking URL
      already in the wild.

      The security property is that a wrong/missing phone suffix and a
      non-existent order return **the same** answer (`null`), so the two
      cannot be told apart. Verified live: `/track/100001`,
      `/track/999999` and `/track/TH-NOPE-123` all return HTTP 200 with
      the same verify form.

      Also: `metadata.title` no longer echoes the order number (it leaked
      into history/referrers before anything was proven), the page is
      `robots: noindex`, and `trackingCode` is only returned after
      verification. New `limitPublicTracking` (existing PostgreSQL bucket
      architecture, no Redis) throttles per IP **and** per queried value
      — the per-key bucket still bites when the spoofable
      `X-Forwarded-For` evades the IP one (F-04).

      **Regression I introduced and caught via the tests:**
      `looksLikeOrderNumber` accepts any bare 5-12 digit string, so an
      11-digit phone satisfies it too. The original code disambiguated by
      attempting the order lookup and falling through to the phone branch
      when it found nothing — a fallback that no longer exists, because a
      lookup without the phone digits now always returns null. My first
      version put the phone branch first, which broke `TH-…` lookups.
      Resolved by shape instead: explicit `TH-…` is always an order, 10+
      digits is a phone, anything else digit-shaped falls through to the
      order branch. Documented in the code.

      Suites: `test:tracking` 11 (new), `test:wallet` 11,
      `test:privilege` 11, `test:inventory` 21, `test:payments` 53,
      `test:security` 1/16 pre-existing. `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors,
      `npm run build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-323 Security remediation Phase 6 — CSRF / API / route security
      (DSA-08 + the full sweep the phase asked for).

      **Sweep result, all 12 route handlers.** Only one real gap. The
      payment callbacks (bkash, nagad, sslcommerz ipn/return) and the
      OAuth routes are *correctly* unauthenticated and must NOT get a
      same-origin check — they are called by the gateway and the provider
      respectively, and are protected by server-side validation and the
      OAuth state cookie. Adding CSRF checks there would break payments.

      **DSA-08 fixed.** `/admin/api/order-alerts` POST now checks the
      origin (403) and requires `application/json` (415), and a malformed
      body is a 400 instead of being treated as "no ids" — which was the
      actual bug, since that made a junk request mark ALL of an admin's
      alerts read.

      **Server actions: 249 exported across 84 files.** 5 mutating
      actions in 4 files lacked the origin check every sibling applies —
      complaint, support, product-request and the two ticket actions.
      All now guarded. The 11 remaining unguarded actions are `load*` /
      `validate*` reads; verified 0 write operations in all 5 files, so
      CSRF does not apply. The new suite asserts that exemption stays
      true — if one of those files ever starts writing, the check fails.

      **F-12 closed too** (it was in the phase's "audit rate limiting"
      remit): the three unauthenticated storefront forms now use a new
      `limitPublicForm` on the existing PostgreSQL bucket architecture.

      **Test-method note.** The live checks first came back 200 for
      everything, which looked like the guards failing. They were never
      consulted: middleware redirects unauthenticated `/admin/*` to the
      login page and `fetch` follows redirects, so every probe measured
      the login page. The suite now mints a real StaffSession row, signs
      the session JWT and sends it as a cookie with `redirect: "manual"`.
      It also asserts the legitimate same-origin request still returns
      200, so the guard cannot degrade into a 403 machine.

      Suites: `test:routes` 12 (new), `test:tracking` 11,
      `test:wallet` 11, `test:privilege` 11, `test:inventory` 21,
      `test:payments` 53, `test:security` 1/16 pre-existing.
      `tsc --noEmit` 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-324 Security remediation Phase 7 — lower-severity hardening
      (DSA-09, DSA-11, DSA-12, DSA-13). DSA-10 and DSA-14 deliberately
      NOT changed — see below.

      **DSA-09** address limit TOCTOU: the count and the insert now run
      in one transaction under `SELECT … FROM "User" … FOR UPDATE`. No
      constraint can express "at most N rows", so serialising on the
      owner is the mechanism.

      **DSA-11** uploads were accepted on extension + browser-declared
      MIME, both attacker-controlled. New `lib/media/file-signature.ts`
      verifies the actual leading bytes and that they match the
      extension, wired into BOTH upload paths — the admin media library
      and the customer-reachable B2B KYC upload — and checked *before*
      anything is written to disk. Allowlist of signatures, so an
      unrecognised format is refused rather than waved through. SVG and
      PDF have no binary magic number (both are text) so they are matched
      structurally, which is weaker — the suite states this explicitly
      and asserts that a scripted SVG still passes, because a signature
      check says what a file IS, not whether it is safe. F-02's serving
      headers remain the control for that.

      **DSA-12** stock ids are now sorted and de-duplicated before the
      `FOR UPDATE` loop, so every checkout takes locks in the same order.
      De-duplication matters since DSA-01 established that several cart
      lines can share one stock row.

      **DSA-13** `normalizeSmtpHost` now refuses loopback, private,
      link-local and cloud-metadata addresses plus `.local` / `.internal`
      names. Documented honestly as best-effort: a public DNS name that
      resolves to a private address still passes, and catching that needs
      resolution at connect time.

      **NOT changed, on purpose** (the brief says not to change intended
      business behaviour without evidence):
      * DSA-10 — a SUSPENDED B2B account can re-apply and reset itself to
        PENDING. Fixing it properly needs an immutable `suspendedAt` /
        `suspensionReason` so the approver keeps the signal; blocking
        re-application outright would stop a reseller fixing genuinely
        bad documents. Needs an operator decision.
      * DSA-14 — coupons have no per-customer cap and no redemption
        table. Whether that is intended is a merchandising decision.

      Suites: `test:hardening` 32 (new), `test:routes` 12,
      `test:tracking` 11, `test:wallet` 11, `test:privilege` 11,
      `test:inventory` 21, `test:payments` 53, `test:security` 1/16
      pre-existing. `tsc --noEmit` 0, `npm run build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-325 Security remediation Phase 15 — security headers (F-01),
      which also closes the serving half of F-02.

      The app sent **no** security headers at all. The concrete
      consequence was that the admin panel could be framed by any site,
      so a signed-in staff member could be tricked through an invisible
      overlay into clicking "Approve refund" or "Ban customer".

      **Audited what the app actually loads before writing any CSP**, as
      the brief requires: 4 INLINE script bootstraps (GTM, GA4, Meta
      Pixel, Tawk.to), 3 external script hosts, self-hosted fonts via
      next/font, and the Custom Scripts box that injects arbitrary
      third-party `<script>` markup by design.

      **So the CSP is split in two, deliberately:**
      * ENFORCED, carrying only `frame-ancestors 'none'` — safe today
        because absent directives are not enforced without a
        `default-src`. This is the clickjacking control and it is live.
      * REPORT-ONLY, carrying the full policy. It cannot be enforced
        while those four inline bootstraps exist; enforcing `script-src`
        without `'unsafe-inline'` would break analytics and chat, and a
        policy that keeps `'unsafe-inline'` buys little. Report-only
        first lets the operator see real violations before anything
        breaks. A test asserts the enforced header does NOT contain
        `default-src`, so the staged policy cannot be switched on by
        accident.

      Also enforced: `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`,
      `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin`,
      `Permissions-Policy` denying camera/mic/geolocation,
      `X-Frame-Options: DENY`. HSTS is **production-only** — sending it
      over plain http would pin the browser to https://localhost and make
      the dev server unreachable until the cache was cleared.

      **F-02 serving half closed:** `/uploads/*` now gets
      `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox` + nosniff, so an uploaded SVG
      navigated to directly can no longer execute its own script.
      `<img>` rendering is unaffected — subresource loads ignore it.
      (The upload-validation half was DSA-11 in AD-324; note a signature
      check alone does NOT stop this, because a scripted SVG is still a
      valid SVG.)

      Verified safe before enabling `X-Frame-Options: DENY`: the app
      contains **no** iframes pointing at its own origin, and
      `window.open`/`window.print` are unaffected. Smoke-tested 11 key
      routes plus an uploaded file — all HTTP 200.

      Suites: `test:headers` 12 (new), and all seven others green.
      Removing the `headers()` block fails 9 of the 12.
      `tsc --noEmit` 0, `npm run build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-326 Security remediation — OAuth identity linking
      (P0-01 HIGH, P0-02, P0-03, P0-05). Not in the operator's original
      phase list; scheduled deliberately because P0-01 was the last open
      HIGH.

      **P0-01 pre-registration account hijack.** Registration never
      verifies the email, so anyone could sign up as victim@example.com.
      When the real owner later signed in with Google, the provider
      correctly attested that *they* own the address — and the code took
      that as licence to link their identity into the attacker's
      pre-registered account, which still had the attacker's password.
      The victim then filled that account with their real name, phone,
      addresses and order history.

      The key insight: **`emailVerified` from the provider proves the
      person signing in owns the address, never that the pre-existing
      account does.** So linking now needs actual proof of control, by
      one of two routes — the visitor is already signed in to that exact
      account, or the account has no password at all (meaning it was
      itself created by a social sign-in, so there is no password-holder
      to impersonate and no pre-registration to hijack). Otherwise the
      attempt is refused with an actionable message and a distinct
      `social_link_required` error code, so the real owner is told to
      sign in with their password first and repeat.

      **P0-02** is closed by the same change: because email alone can no
      longer link, it no longer matters that Facebook exposes no
      verification claim and the adapter infers one from the presence of
      an email.

      **P0-03 social sign-up was broken end to end.** OAuth created users
      with `passwordHash: null`, and `getCustomerSession` treated a null
      hash as "disabled" and revoked every session on the next request.
      Fixed by distinguishing "no credential" from "no password": a
      linked provider identity now counts. Deliberately NOT fixed by
      deleting the check, which would also have let genuinely
      unprovisioned accounts through.

      **P0-05** `emailVerifiedAt` is now set when a provider attests the
      address. Nothing in the codebase had ever written it, which is why
      the admin's "Verified" customer filter was permanently empty.

      **Design change worth keeping:** `completeSocialLogin` originally
      read the current session from cookies itself. That made it depend
      on ambient request state — untestable outside a request and an
      invisible dependency for callers. The callback route now resolves
      it and passes `currentUserId` explicitly.

      Suites: `test:social` 12 (new). Removing the guards fails 5 of 12,
      including `linked=1` — the victim's identity attached to the
      attacker's account. All nine other suites green. `tsc --noEmit` 0,
      `npm run build` exit 0.

      **Known UX consequence:** a customer who registered with a password
      and later wants to use Google must sign in with the password first,
      then click the social button. There is no "link account" screen in
      settings; building one would remove that step.

- [x] AD-327 Security remediation — trustworthy client IP (F-04).

      The client address keys every rate-limit bucket and every `ipHash`
      written to a session or audit row. `getRequestMeta` took the
      **left-most** `X-Forwarded-For` entry, which is exactly backwards:
      a proxy APPENDS the address it received from, so a forged header
      arrives as `1.2.3.4, <real address>`. The left-most entry is the
      one part the client fully controls; the right-most is the only part
      the proxy vouches for.

      Consequence: rotating the forged prefix minted a fresh bucket per
      request, defeating every `*.ip` limit — including the tracking and
      public-form limits added in AD-322 and AD-323. It also meant audit
      rows recorded whatever the attacker typed.

      New `lib/auth/client-ip.ts` reads from the RIGHT by
      `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS` (default 1 = one reverse proxy, the normal
      nginx/Caddy/Vercel shape; 0 = directly exposed, so XFF is ignored
      entirely). Platform headers that the edge overwrites
      (`x-vercel-forwarded-for`, `cf-connecting-ip`, `true-client-ip`)
      take precedence and are trusted even at 0 hops. `getRequestMeta`
      keeps its exact signature, so none of its 70 call sites changed.

      Setting the hop count too HIGH is safe (it reads a proxy address);
      too LOW lets a client forge their own. Documented in `.env.example`
      with the reasoning, since it is deployment topology and code cannot
      infer it.

      Suite `test:clientip` — 23 pure input/output checks, no database or
      server needed. Reverting to the left-most read fails 5, including
      the decisive one: three rotated forgeries producing three different
      buckets.

      **Note for local development:** with no proxy and no XFF, the
      resolved IP is now `null` rather than a fabricated value, so local
      requests share one rate-limit bucket. Correct, and harmless for a
      single developer, but worth knowing if local testing ever hits a
      limit unexpectedly.

      All eleven suites green. `tsc --noEmit` 0, `npm run build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-328 Security remediation — staff lockout (F-05) and password
      policy (F-07).

      **F-05.** Staff sign-in had no lockout. The only brake was a sliding
      15-minute rate-limit window, which forgets everything once it
      expires — so a patient attacker could keep guessing indefinitely.
      Added `Staff.failedLoginAttempts` and `Staff.lockedUntil`
      (migration `20260913..._staff_login_lockout`): 10 consecutive
      failures locks the account for 30 minutes, and a successful sign-in
      resets the counter.

      Two details that matter:
      * The lock is checked **after** Argon2 verification, not before, so
        a locked account costs an attacker exactly the same time as any
        other and the response cannot be used to discover which accounts
        exist or which are locked.
      * The counter resets on success. Without that the threshold would
        be cumulative over an account's whole life and would eventually
        lock out someone who merely mistypes occasionally — which is how
        lockouts become a denial of service against your own staff.

      **F-07.** Policy was length-only, so `password`, `12345678` and
      `technohouse` all passed at 8 characters. New
      `lib/account/weak-passwords.ts` rejects a common-password list plus
      structural shapes (single repeated character, keyboard/alphabet
      runs, the shop's own name, and the account's own email local-part
      or name). Deliberately offline and dependency-free: the Pwned
      Passwords API is stronger coverage but puts a third-party HTTP call
      on the registration path, where it can be slow, be down, or
      silently degrade to no check at all.

      **REGRESSION I NEARLY SHIPPED, and the lesson.** `validateLoginInput`
      also called `validatePassword`, so the new strength rules would have
      run at **sign-in** — locking every existing user whose password is
      now considered weak out of their own account, with no recovery,
      because password reset is not implemented (F-16). `tsc` caught it
      only incidentally, as a missing-property error on an unrelated
      field. Sign-in now checks that a password was **supplied** and
      nothing more; strength belongs to registration and password change.
      Three tests were added specifically to pin this: a legacy weak
      password and a legacy short password must both still sign in.

      **General rule worth carrying forward: password strength rules must
      never run on the sign-in path.** They lock out exactly the people
      the rule was meant to protect, and they leak policy to an
      unauthenticated caller.

      Suite `test:authhardening` — 31 checks. Removing both fixes fails
      19. All twelve suites green. `tsc --noEmit` 0, `npm run build`
      exit 0.

- [x] AD-329 Security remediation — at-rest secret key derivation (F-06).

      Three modules encrypt operator-supplied credentials — payment
      gateway, object storage, courier API — and all three derived their
      AES key identically:

          createHash("sha256").update(passphrase).digest()   // 16-char min

      The AES-256-GCM around it was always fine. The derivation was the
      weak part: SHA-256 is built to be fast, so an attacker holding a
      database backup could grind a human-chosen 16-character passphrase
      cheaply, and with no salt one precomputation served every
      installation **and all three purposes at once**. What that protects
      is live merchant credentials.

      New `lib/security/secret-key.ts`, shared by all three (they were
      triplicated, which is how you fix one and miss two):
      * A **full-entropy key is used directly** — 64 hex chars, or base64
        decoding to >= 32 bytes. That is what production should set.
      * Anything else is stretched with **scrypt** (N=2^14) using a
        **per-purpose salt**, so the same passphrase no longer yields the
        same key for gateway, storage and courier.
      * Production requires >= 32 characters and the refusal message
        includes `openssl rand -base64 48`. Development keeps the
        16-character floor.

      **Backward compatibility:** decryption tries the modern key, then
      falls back to the legacy SHA-256 key. GCM's auth tag makes that
      unambiguous — a wrong key fails to verify rather than returning
      garbage — so the fallback is safe. The legacy key is never used to
      encrypt, so any secret re-saved after this change is upgraded in
      place. A test encrypts with the old key and asserts it still opens.

      **Checked before changing anything:** 0 PaymentGatewaySetting rows,
      0 CourierSetting rows with ciphertext, and none of the three keys
      configured — so there was nothing to migrate. The fallback was built
      anyway, because production may differ from this database.

      Suite `test:secretcrypto` — 21 checks, pure input/output. Reverting
      to the unsalted SHA-256 derivation fails 5. All thirteen suites
      green. `tsc --noEmit` 0, `npm run build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-330 Security remediation — F-03, P0-04, lint and baseline
      cleanup. The last two Mediums plus the housekeeping.

      **F-03 open redirect.** `safeReturnPath` rejected a leading `//` and
      any `://`, which looks thorough and is not: browsers parse URLs by
      WHATWG rules where a BACKSLASH is equivalent to a forward slash for
      http(s), so `/\evil.com` cleared every check and then resolved to
      `https://evil.com/`. It starts with `/`, does not start with `//`,
      and contains no `://`.

      Replaced the blocklist with positive validation: resolve the
      candidate against `https://return-path.invalid` and require the
      origin to have survived, then rebuild the path from the parsed URL
      so the value handed to `redirect()` is the one actually validated.
      Anything that escapes the origin now fails identically, whatever
      the parser quirk. The original `://` rejection was KEPT as belt and
      braces — `/account/https://evil.com` is genuinely same-origin and
      safe, but no route here contains `://`, and the existing baseline
      asserted that rejection deliberately.

      **I had mis-stated this one.** Two phases earlier I wrote "no High
      or Medium findings remain from the original audits". F-03 was open
      the whole time — I folded it into a "F-03 … F-16" range in the
      remaining-findings lists and never singled it out. Corrected to the
      operator directly.

      **P0-04.** Sign-in gates the OTP challenge on
      `otpConfig.otpLogin && user.phone`, which is the only sensible
      behaviour at that point — there is nowhere to send a code — but it
      means enabling the policy never makes it universal, and nothing
      surfaced that. New `countOtpExemptCustomers()`; the Admin -> OTP
      screen now states plainly how many active customers will keep
      signing in with a password only. Deliberately NOT fixed by refusing
      sign-in without a second factor: that locks out existing accounts
      with no recovery path, the same trap as the password-policy
      regression in AD-328.

      **Lint is green.** 13 errors -> 0.
      * 7 unescaped entities: escaped.
      * Pathao modal: the cascade reset AND the loading flags moved from
        effects into the handler that causes them — state resets belong
        to the event, not to an effect reacting to it afterwards.
      * Media picker: the parent now mounts it only while open, so it
        starts fresh instead of resetting itself in an effect; the SSR
        mount guard moved to `useSyncExternalStore`, matching
        `site-alert.tsx`.
      * Alert bell: ONE justified `eslint-disable` — polling with setState
        inside an async callback is the shape React's own guidance
        sanctions, and the rule cannot see through `void refresh()`.
        Letting the interval fire the first fetch would blank the bell for
        a full POLL_MS on every page load. Recorded as a suppression, not
        a fix.

      **`test:security` is green for the first time** (17 checks). Its
      blanket `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` ban had been failing for months
      because six legitimate sinks were added after it was written — a
      permanently-red check stops being a check. Replaced with an
      allowlist where each entry records WHY that sink is safe, plus a
      stale-entry check so the list cannot rot. Verified by adding a new
      sink: it is named and fails, and passes once removed.

      **`writeAuditLog` inside `$transaction`: clean.** Brace-depth scan
      of the four candidate files found every call already outside the
      transaction. `save-customer.ts` (AD-321) was the only offender.

      First time all of these are green together: 13 suites (282 checks),
      `tsc --noEmit` 0, `npm run lint` 0 errors, `npm run build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-331 Phase 17 — admin functionality verification. AUDIT ONLY, no
      code changed. Report: `ADMIN_FUNCTIONALITY_VERIFICATION.md`.

      The prior engagement's `ADMIN_FUNCTIONALITY_AUDIT.md` states all
      ~140 admin routes are "real, database-backed, and auth-gated". The
      auth-gating and the persistence both hold. But **database-backed
      and functional are not the same claim**, and for one settings group
      they come apart.

      **F17-01 (Medium, business impact): Store Operations is
      persist-only.** `/admin/settings/orders` plus tax, pickup points,
      invoice, order tracking, shipping label and thermal printer all
      validate, save, audit-log and reload correctly — and nothing else
      in the application reads any of it.
      `getStoreOperationsSettings()` has zero consumers outside the admin
      panel. The proof is `create-order.ts:426`, `const taxAmount = 0;` —
      a VAT rate saved in admin has **no effect on any order total**. The
      save path is not broken; the read path was never written. The risk
      is that the screens look authoritative.

      **F17-02 (Low):** currency format settings likewise persist-only —
      `formatMoney` uses a fixed symbol constant.

      **F17-03 (Info):** `getOtpFeatureFlags` is dead — zero consumers
      anywhere, including admin. `getAdminOtpConfig` is the real one.

      **F17-04 (Low):** the delivery-boy assignment DOES persist, but by
      writing a `Delivery: <name>` line into `Order.staffNotes` and
      parsing it back. The roster itself is `MOCK_DELIVERY_BOYS` — four
      hardcoded fictional names, so an operator cannot add real riders —
      and there is no `deliveryBoy` column, so notes edits can corrupt it
      and it cannot be reported on.

      **Verified sound:** all 165 pages carry a permission rule (unmapped
      routes deny by default; there are none). 13 of the 14 runtime
      `*-mock` imports are helper functions and constants that merely
      live in badly-named files — the code behind them is real.

      **Method correction worth keeping.** My first settings scan omitted
      `components/` and reported "12 of 29 settings have no effect".
      Wrong — `getStorefrontAnalyticsTags` is consumed from
      `components/analytics/`. Several others are read through a wrapper
      that a grep for the admin getter misses. The report lists those
      false positives explicitly so nobody repeats the bad scan and
      "rediscovers" them.

- [x] AD-332 Security remediation — remaining low-severity findings
      (F-10, F-13, F-14, F-15, F-16 actionable parts). Decision-free
      engineering only; the outstanding business questions were left
      alone.

      **F-10** `/b2b/:path*` added to the middleware matcher. All twelve
      wholesale pages already guard themselves correctly — but `/account`
      and `/admin` also get a structural gate and `/b2b` did not, and the
      panel exposes negotiated pricing and order history. A page added
      later without its own guard now fails closed. Unauthenticated
      wholesale requests redirect to `/b2b/login` (not `/account/login`)
      with the return path preserved; `/b2b/login` and `/b2b/register`
      stay public. Verified live across 8 routes.

      **F-13** `absoluteFromPublicPath` now uses `path.relative` instead
      of `startsWith`. `path.join` already collapsed `..`, so traversal
      was handled; what a bare prefix test missed was a SIBLING directory
      sharing the prefix — `<cwd>/publicdata/x` starts with
      `<cwd>/public` and passed.

      **F-14** `getAdminLoginSlug()` throws in production when
      `ADMIN_LOGIN_SLUG` is unset or invalid, instead of silently falling
      back to `th-ops-local` — a value published in `.env.example`, in
      `docs/`, and in this repository's history. Matches how
      `SESSION_JWT_SECRET` already behaves. **Deployment note: the
      variable must be set before the next production deploy or the admin
      panel will not start.** That is the intent — silent obscurity the
      operator believes they have and do not is worse than none.

      **F-15** `.gitignore` negation moved below the broad `.env*`
      pattern, which had been re-ignoring `.env.example`.

      **F-16 (partial)** `consumeAll` now returns on the first refusal.
      Continuing burned a count from every remaining bucket for a request
      already being rejected, so hammering one spoofable IP bucket also
      consumed the caller's per-email budget.

      **Left undone, deliberately:** password reset (a feature, not a
      fix), the `__Host-` cookie prefix (conflicts with the deliberate
      `Path=/admin` scoping on the staff cookie), and OTP codes hashed
      with plain SHA-256 (a 6-digit code has a million possibilities, so
      the hash adds little; mitigated by expiry, single use and the
      attempt counter).

      Suite `test:hardening` now 54 checks. Reverting all five fails 6.
      All 13 suites green, `tsc --noEmit` 0, `npm run lint` 0 errors,
      `npm run build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-333 F17-01 — the admin VAT rate now reaches order totals.

      `create-order.ts` hard-coded `const taxAmount = 0`, so the VAT rate
      in Admin -> Settings validated, saved, audit-logged and reloaded
      correctly while having **no effect on any order total**. The screen
      looked authoritative and was not. Found in the Phase 17 audit.

      **Chosen because it needed no business decision.** The stored
      default is `vatRateBasisPoints: 0`, so wiring it changes nothing
      for anyone until the operator actually sets a rate — the zero-rate
      case is asserted to reproduce the previous behaviour exactly.

      New pure `lib/business/tax.ts`. The important distinction is
      `taxAmount` vs `addedToTotal`:
      * **Inclusive** (`taxIncludedInPrice: true`, the default): the
        displayed price already contains VAT, so the tax is EXTRACTED for
        the invoice and `addedToTotal` is 0. Adding it again would
        overcharge every order by the rate — the most expensive possible
        mistake in this change, so it has its own assertion in both
        suites.
      * **Exclusive**: VAT is added on top.

      Settings are read **before** the transaction opens, deliberately:
      `getStoreOperationsSettings` uses the pooled global client, so
      calling it inside the transaction would request a second connection
      from the `max: 5` pool while holding the first — the exact shape
      that deadlocked the wallet path in AD-321. A test asserts the
      ordering so it cannot be moved back.

      **Stated, not buried:** tax applies to the discounted goods value
      and excludes shipping. That is the common arrangement but it is a
      jurisdiction question, and the code says so at the point of
      decision.

      **Service charge NOT wired** — `Order` has no column for it, and
      folding it into `taxAmount` would be a lie in the ledger. Needs a
      schema field; flagged rather than bodged.

      Suites: `test:tax` 17 new pure checks; `test:inventory` 21 -> 27
      with four end-to-end checks that place REAL orders in both modes
      and restore the settings singleton afterwards. Reverting the fix
      fails 2 in each suite (`subtotal=2000 taxAmount=0`). All 14 suites
      green, `tsc` 0, `lint` 0, `build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-334 DSA-14 — per-customer coupon cap, built as an opt-in
      capability.

      `Coupon.usageLimit` caps the TOTAL across everyone; there was no
      way to stop one account reusing a coupon indefinitely, and a
      percentage coupon with no global limit was an unbounded discount.

      **Built the mechanism, not a policy.** New `Coupon.perUserLimit`
      defaults to `null` = no cap = exactly today's behaviour, so nothing
      changes until the operator sets a number. Same approach that made
      the VAT wiring safe (AD-333). The operator decides the rule; the
      code just makes it expressible. A test asserts an uncapped coupon
      is still reusable by the same customer.

      New `CouponRedemption` table (couponId, userId, orderId unique)
      written in the SAME transaction as the order and the `usageCount`
      increment, so the ledger cannot drift from the counter.
      `usageCount` alone cannot answer "has THIS customer used it
      before?", which is why a ledger was needed rather than another
      counter.

      **Concurrency:** `checkCouponPerUserLimit` takes
      `SELECT … FROM "Coupon" … FOR UPDATE` before counting. Counting and
      then inserting is a read-then-act — without the lock two concurrent
      checkouts by the same customer both count the same total, both
      pass, and the cap is exceeded. That is the identical shape to the
      refund (DSA-04) and wallet (DSA-06) races. The lock is held to
      commit, so the count cannot move between the check and the insert
      that follows order creation. An uncapped coupon short-circuits
      BEFORE taking the lock, so it pays nothing for a feature it does
      not use.

      Admin → Coupons gains a "Per-customer limit" field with helper text
      spelling out the distinction from the usage limit, since the two
      are easy to confuse.

      Migration `20260913154048_coupon_per_user_limit` — purely additive:
      one nullable column, one new table. Nothing altered.

      Suite `test:inventory` 27 -> 33 with six checks that place REAL
      orders: first use succeeds, redemption recorded, same customer
      refused with an explanatory message, a DIFFERENT customer
      unaffected, and an uncapped coupon still reusable. Disabling the
      cap fails 2. All 14 suites green (318 checks), `tsc` 0, `lint` 0,
      `build` exit 0.

- [x] AD-335 The four items that had been waiting on a decision — decided and
      built.

      **DSA-10 — a suspended wholesale account could clear its own
      suspension.** `applyForB2B` allowed re-application from `SUSPENDED` and
      reset the row to `PENDING` with `approvedAt: null`. The account left the
      "suspended" tab and re-entered the staff review queue looking like an
      ordinary new application, so whoever worked that queue would approve it
      back to ACTIVE without ever seeing it had been suspended. The subject of
      a penalty decided when the penalty ended.

      The audit had recommended keeping an immutable `suspendedAt` and
      surfacing it to the approver. The simpler fix closes the hole instead of
      annotating it: **no existing account re-applies, in any state.**
      `SUSPENDED` is written by exactly one path — `setB2BAccountSuspended`, a
      staff action — and the only way back to ACTIVE is a staff member lifting
      it, so this was never a legitimate transition. The re-apply update branch
      is gone entirely rather than left disabled. The check-then-create race is
      closed by the existing `userId` unique constraint with P2002 handled; the
      pre-check only exists to produce a good message.

      **Stale-order sweeper.** Stock is reserved when the order row is written,
      before the customer reaches the gateway. AD-3xx (DSA-02) gave every
      *resolved* order a release path; nothing released the order that never
      resolves. Every abandoned checkout lowered a product's availability
      permanently — a lost sale that causes more lost sales.

      Window **24h**, `STALE_ORDER_RELEASE_HOURS` to override. Every hosted
      session expires inside an hour, so 24h cannot collide with a customer who
      is still paying. An invalid value falls BACK to 24 rather than clamping:
      a typo of "0" clamped to 1 would sweep checkouts an hour old.

      **Selection is positive** — the order must have a payment row for a
      hosted gateway. A COD order is legitimately unpaid for days and is never
      a candidate; an order with no payment row is never a candidate. Wrong
      selection then fails by leaving stock held, which is the old behaviour,
      not by cancelling real orders.

      The order is **cancelled**, not merely un-reserved. Releasing stock while
      leaving it PENDING is worse: `convertOrderStock` only converts from
      RESERVED, so a later "delivered" would never decrement `quantity` and the
      ledger would drift. Stock state and order state move together.

      **Lock order matters.** Payment rows are locked first, then the order is
      written — the same order `applyPaymentTransition` uses. Reversing them
      gives two paths taking the same two locks in opposite orders, which
      deadlocks the day a webhook lands during a sweep. Holding that lock is
      also what makes the re-check meaningful.

      A late payment cannot revive a swept order: CANCELLED is terminal, so the
      webhook is refused and a person looks at the money. Correct — those units
      may already be sold.

      No cron in this project, so: `npm run orders:stale` (dry run; `--apply`
      mandatory to write) and `POST /api/internal/sweep-stale-orders` behind
      `STALE_SWEEP_TOKEN`, 404 when unset, constant-time compare, POST only.

      **Rest of F17-01.** Split by whether wiring was a missing call or a
      product decision. Wired, each with a no-op default: **service charge**
      (its own `Order.serviceChargeAmount` column — folding it into `taxAmount`
      or `shippingAmount` would make the invoice and any VAT return lie),
      **minimum order amount** (server-side; measured before the coupon so a
      valid discount cannot reject a qualifying cart at the final step), and
      **auto-confirm paid orders** (guarded on `status: PENDING` so a late
      webhook cannot drag a shipped order back to Processing).

      The checkout summary now derives its total with the SAME pure functions
      the server charges with. It previously computed `subtotal − discount +
      shipping` independently, so the VAT wiring had already left it quoting
      one figure and charging another whenever exclusive VAT was on.

      `orderCodePrefix` deliberately NOT wired: order numbers are bare
      sequential digits by explicit design, and the prefix defaults to "TH-",
      so wiring it would change every future order number's format with nobody
      asking. Inline "Not applied" hint instead. Pickup, invoice, tracking,
      label and printer each get a visible "Saved but not yet applied" notice —
      a screen that saves successfully is indistinguishable from one that
      works.

      Migration `20260913190000_order_service_charge` — additive: one column
      with default 0 (catalogue-only in Postgres, no heap rewrite) plus a
      non-negative CHECK.

      New suite `test:staleorders` (32). `test:tax` 17 -> 35, `test:hardening`
      54 -> 60. Every guard proved load-bearing by reverting it: removing both
      COD guards cancels a four-day-old COD order and returns its stock;
      removing the age window sweeps a two-hour-old order; weakening the
      under-lock re-check releases a non-candidate.

      **Caught a false pass in my own test**: the B2B fixture used junk bytes
      for the uploaded PNG, so with the guard deleted the application was still
      refused — by `verifyFileSignature`, not by the guard. Real PNG bytes now,
      and the assertion checks the refusal REASON.

      All 16 suites green (380 checks), `tsc` 0, `lint` 0 errors, `build` 0.

- [x] AD-336 Phase 20 — final verification. `REMEDIATION_VERIFICATION.md`.

      The only code written for this phase was the document. Everything in it
      was re-checked today rather than written from memory, because a
      verification document assembled from recollection is worth nothing:
      constraints queried from `pg_constraint`/`pg_indexes`, invariants counted
      against the live tables, every fix located by grepping for the actual
      guard rather than the finding ID (a comment naming a finding is not a
      fix), and all 16 suites re-run with the dev server up.

      Result: 16/16 suites, 382 checks, `tsc` 0, `lint` 0 errors, `build` 0.
      Live DB: 0 stock rows outside the reserved window, 0 negative wallets,
      0 negative service charges, 0 orders with two open refunds. The database
      now refuses these states rather than trusting the application not to
      produce them — which is the point, since the application layer is where
      every one of the bugs was.

      Documented honestly rather than tidily:

      - **F-01 is partial.** Only `frame-ancestors` is enforced; the full CSP
        is Report-Only and cannot be enforced without a report-collection
        period and an exception for Custom Scripts (F-09).
      - **F17-02/03/04 remain open**, and F17-04 (delivery roster hardcoded and
        stored inside free-text `staffNotes`) has real operational cost.
      - **The sweep does not run itself.** If nobody schedules it the stock fix
        is inert and abandoned checkouts resume accumulating. Called out as a
        deployment requirement, not a footnote.
      - **The same agent wrote the fixes and the verification.** Stated as a
        limitation, with the mitigation named: every claim is backed by a
        reproducible command, and every guard was proved load-bearing by
        breaking it.

      Also recorded the two false passes and the near-miss lockout regression,
      because the lesson generalises: a test asserting only "it failed" passes
      for the wrong reason. Assert why.

      Three live-dependent suites skip silently with no dev server
      (`payments` 53->51, `routes` 12->8, `headers` 12->0) — so CI must check
      the count, not just the exit code.

      NOT run: the sweeper's `--apply`. Its dry run found 4 real abandoned
      orders in the dev database; cancelling them is a data operation awaiting
      explicit approval.

- [x] AD-337 Production readiness & hardening pre-deployment phase.

      Read-only audit first, then only changes with a concrete reason. No
      architecture was touched: JWT, sessions, RBAC, Prisma, the payment state
      machine and the stock reservation design are all unchanged.

      **The find that mattered.** F-14 made production throw when
      `ADMIN_LOGIN_SLUG` was missing. It validated the slug's SHAPE, so a
      well-formed value passed — including `th-ops-local`, the default printed
      in `.env.example`, in `docs/`, and in the source. Copying the example
      file is how deployments are normally configured, so the likely mistake
      was never the forgotten variable that guard caught; it was the copied
      one. Proved it against a real production build: `NODE_ENV=production
      npm start` came up happily and redirected `/admin` to
      `/admin/access/th-ops-local`. Production now refuses that specific value.
      Storefront and `/api/health` stay 200 when it fires — only the admin
      gate 500s, which is the right blast radius.

      **Deployment doc was dangerously stale.** Dated before the remediation
      and never mentioned `ADMIN_LOGIN_SLUG` — a variable that now halts
      startup. A deploy following it would have failed. Also missing: the three
      `*_SECRETS_KEY` vars, `TRUSTED_PROXY_HOPS`, the stale-order scheduler
      (without which a fixed inventory defect silently returns), migration
      procedure, backups, rollback, health check, smoke tests. Rewritten.

      **Corrected my own error** in `REMEDIATION_VERIFICATION.md`: it named
      `SECRET_ENCRYPTION_KEY`, which does not exist. The real ones are
      `GATEWAY_SECRETS_KEY` / `STORAGE_SECRETS_KEY` / `COURIER_SECRETS_KEY` —
      three separate values with per-purpose salts, not one shared key. A
      deployer following that line would have set a variable nothing reads.

      **Added:** `/api/health` (SELECT 1, so it distinguishes "listening" from
      "can serve"; deliberately returns no version or migration state, since it
      is unauthenticated by necessity) and `npm run db:preflight` — 11
      read-only invariants, safe against production, repairs nothing.

      **A test was asserting the wrong environment.** `test:headers` checked
      "HSTS absent" guarded on the TEST process's `NODE_ENV`, not the probed
      server's, so it failed against a correct production build and never once
      asserted HSTS was actually present. Now asserts both directions via
      `PROBE_ENV`; 12 -> 14 checks.

      **Reported, not fixed:** `/admin/login` and wrong slugs return 200 with
      the not-found BODY, because the admin layout is `force-dynamic` and the
      response streams before `notFound()` runs. No form is rendered, so
      nothing is exposed — but WAF rules that count 404s will not see them.
      Handled as Cloudflare configuration (rate-limit by request count, not
      status) rather than by changing rendering right before a deploy.

      Every new guard proved load-bearing by reverting it. 405 checks green,
      `tsc` 0, `lint` 0 errors, `build` 0, production start verified.

- [x] AD-338 Order ID consistency — `Order.number` made the single
      customer-facing identifier.

      Audit found ⚠️ partial: new orders were numeric and the number was
      already carried correctly through payment, callbacks, refunds, tracking,
      invoices and courier — but ADMIN URLs and the audit log exposed
      `Order.id` (cuid) instead, and nothing enforced the numeric format.

      **Admin URLs now quote the number.** Staff and customer look at the same
      reference; previously staff saw
      `/admin/orders/cmtouibib0007hsfgg7dw33ta` for order `100187`. Six
      components plus `invoiceHref`. `revalidatePath` had to move with them —
      revalidating the cuid path after the links changed would have silently
      missed, leaving a stale page after every edit. `updateAdminOrder` now
      returns `number` alongside `id` for that.

      Server-action payloads still pass `order.id`. Those are internal, not
      URLs, and `Order.id` remains the primary key as approved.

      **Audit log.** Order entries carry `orderNumber` (courier had none;
      admin-orders used the key `number` — standardised). The view prefers it
      and falls back to the cuid for historical rows, marked "(internal id)".
      Nothing was backfilled: audit history is evidence, and rewriting it to
      look tidier destroys the thing that makes it evidence.

      **Numeric enforcement at two layers.** `nextOrderNumber` refuses a
      non-numeric value before it reaches the database, so a future generator
      change fails with a message instead of a constraint violation. The real
      guard is `Order_number_format`:
      `number ~ '^[0-9]+$' OR number LIKE 'TH-%'`.

      The `TH-%` arm is the point, not a loophole. Eight legacy orders are
      recorded at the gateways as `tran_id` and printed on invoices;
      renumbering them would break every one of those references. Verified
      BEFORE writing the migration that all 9 rows already satisfy the
      predicate, so Postgres's validation scan touches nothing.

      **Second generator deleted.** `createMockOrderId()` produced
      `TH-<base36>` and was called from the real checkout view under
      `DATA_SOURCE=mock`. Mock mode now gets `placeCustomerOrder`'s existing
      refusal instead of an invented order id.

      New suite `test:orderid` (41). Every guard proved load-bearing by
      reverting it — including DROPping the constraint, which made the two
      database checks fail, then re-adding it. 446 checks green, `tsc` 0,
      `lint` 0 errors, `build` 0.

- [x] AD-339 Admin Appearance phases 1-3 — storefront theme, admin-editable.

      **Phase 1 (hardening, no visible change).** `getAdminThemeSettings`
      wrapped in React `cache()` — six call sites read it and the storefront
      layout is one, so uncached meant a DB round trip per page render.
      Emitter turned into a serialization boundary: a fixed token table decides
      property names, and every value is re-validated against `#rrggbb` at
      RENDER time, not just at save. Save-time validation runs on the write;
      the render check is what covers a row edited in psql or restored from a
      backup. Proved load-bearing — without it,
      `#fff;}</style><script>alert(1)</script>` lands verbatim in the injected
      style block.

      Invalid or empty falls back by emitting NOTHING, so the value compiled
      into globals.css applies. Substituting a hardcoded fallback would
      duplicate the defaults in a second place and let them drift.

      **Phase 2 (schema).** Six nullable columns, each driving a CSS variable
      that ALREADY exists in globals.css and is already consumed by Tailwind
      utilities — which is why no component changed and none needed to.
      Tailwind v4 compiles every utility to `var(--color-…)`, so a `:root`
      override retints the whole storefront for free. Verified in the built
      CSS before relying on it.

      Subtlety that needed care: new fields are OPTIONAL. The existing
      Appearance form submitted only four colours, so if "omitted" meant
      "clear", every save from it would wipe the six new ones. `colorPatch`
      separates undefined (leave alone) from "" (clear). Test pins it.

      **Phase 3 (admin UI).** Appearance page now renders from the token
      table — adding a token is the only edit needed to expose a colour.
      Grouped Brand / Text & surfaces / Buttons, per-field reset, live preview,
      contrast warnings.

      The preview is real storefront markup under scoped CSS variables, not a
      mock-up, so it cannot drift from what saving produces. It also shows
      status badges labelled "not themeable" — rebranding must never disguise
      an out-of-stock warning.

      **Two real bugs found while building it.** (a) The client component
      imported a VALUE from the Prisma-importing module and dragged `pg` into
      the browser bundle — build failed; fixed by extracting the pure token
      table to `lib/design/theme-tokens.ts`. (b) `AdminThemeSettingsProp` was a
      hand-written structural copy of `AdminThemeSettings` that had already
      fallen behind; aliased it so the next added column is a compile error
      rather than a silently missing field.

      Defaults are duplicated in the token table so the UI can show and restore
      them — made safe by a test that PARSES globals.css and asserts all ten
      still match. Drifting one digit fails it.

      Status colours are asserted NOT themeable. `test:theme` 0 -> 134. All 17
      suites green (560), `tsc` 0, `lint` 0 errors, `build` 0, page rendered
      with a real staff session.

      **Phase 4 (Track Order migration).** Seven lines across four files
      carried `#1d6fd8` / `#185bb3` — a near-miss blue, close enough to look
      deliberate and different enough to drift. A hardcoded hex is invisible to
      the theme: the admin could change Primary and those buttons would stay
      the old colour.

      NOT a no-op, and the earlier audit calling it one was imprecise. Measured
      every pairing first: white on button 4.86 -> 5.83, hover 6.59 -> 8.47,
      link on page 4.52 -> 5.43, link on card 4.86 -> 5.83. Every one improves,
      and the old link colour was barely over AA.

      Removed the overrides rather than substituting tokens. `Button`'s primary
      variant already carries bg-primary/hover:bg-primary-hover, and `cn()` is
      a plain join with NO tailwind-merge — so an override and the variant both
      ended up in the class list with stylesheet order deciding. Deleting the
      override is smaller and unambiguous.

      Proved end to end: setting themeBaseColor to #d62828 injects
      `:root{--color-primary: #d62828;}` and the Track button, now `bg-primary`,
      follows it. It could not before.

      Anti-regression test written with plain substring checks, not a regex —
      the thing forbidden is Tailwind's `[#` arbitrary-value syntax, and a
      regex for it needs escaping that is easy to get subtly wrong in a test
      whose whole job is reliability. `test:theme` 134 -> 142. Reverting one
      link fails 2. All 17 suites green (568).

      **Phase 5 (header/footer tokens).** The dark chrome is FOUR elements —
      top bar, header, category nav, footer — and all sat on `bg-text`, the
      BODY INK. Phase 2 made that admin-editable, so changing paragraph colour
      would silently have retinted the whole chrome. Four new tokens split
      them, defaulting to the exact navy/white they already rendered as.

      `--color-text` keeps its other role deliberately: dark panels OUTSIDE the
      chrome (the `ink` button, auth aside, checkout summary bands) still
      follow the text colour. Tests assert both directions — chrome files must
      NOT use `bg-text`, and those three files must still use it. Otherwise
      "header background" quietly becomes "every dark surface".

      48 `primary-foreground` uses migrated across the four files, but NOT
      blanket: two are buttons on `bg-primary` (header search, footer
      newsletter) whose label must keep following the BUTTON token. A global
      replace would have broken that invisibly until someone set a dark button
      text colour. Pinned by a test.

      Proved live: setting only the header background emits only
      `--color-header-background`; setting only body text emits only
      `--color-text`. Independently themeable, as intended.

      **Two false alarms I chased rather than assumed.** (a) The live e2e first
      showed the chrome override not applying — cause was ME rebuilding `.next`
      underneath the running server, not a defect; a clean rebuild+restart gave
      correct results. (b) The admin page probe reported every new field
      MISSING — the probe's `findFirst` had grabbed a leftover
      `_tmp-verify-staff-…` Support account with no `design_studio.view`, so it
      was reading a permission-denied page. Re-probed as Admin: all 16 markers
      found.

      `test:theme` 142 -> 182. All 17 suites green (608), `tsc` 0, `lint` 0
      errors, `build` 0, theme row all NULL, 23 -> 27 columns.

      **Phase 6 (verification).** The real gap was AUTHORIZATION — never tested
      across five phases. Two independent gates, now both covered: the route
      gate (`design_studio.view`, tested as a pure function so no staff or role
      data is touched) and the action gate (`design_studio.manage`).

      Strengthened a weak check of my own: the per-action assertion originally
      searched the whole FILE, so it would pass while a sibling action sat
      unguarded. Now each body is sliced between `export async function`
      boundaries and checked individually. All five theme actions carry both
      `guard()` and their own permission check — verified, not assumed.

      Proved the festival use case end to end: a full 14-token palette applied
      across `/`, `/cart` and `/track`; ZERO semantic tokens overridden; status
      classes still present; no markup in the emitted block.

      Also covered: reset-all clears every column; the admin rows stack on
      narrow screens; controls are labelled and `aria-invalid` is set; and the
      app is asserted light-only, so if dark mode is added later this check
      fails and the theme work is revisited deliberately.

      **Two self-inflicted incidents, both caught and repaired.** Windows
      Python's `/tmp` is `C:	mp`, bash's is elsewhere — so (a) a backup
      "restore" silently failed and left `admin-route-permissions.ts` WITHOUT
      the design-studio rule, and (b) a Python restore truncated
      `theme-actions.ts` before its read threw. Both restored from the bash
      backups and re-verified. Lesson: one tool for backup and restore, never
      two.

      `test:theme` 182 -> 214. All 17 suites green (646), `tsc` 0, `lint` 0
      errors, `build` 0.

- [x] AD-340 R2 fixed — courier handoff left the admin order page stale.

      `courier-actions.ts` revalidated `/admin/orders/${input.orderId}` — the

      AD-338. So sending an order to Steadfast or Pathao saved `trackingCode`
      and `carrierId` correctly, then revalidated a path nobody visits: staff
      refreshed the order and did not see the tracking code they had just

      `SendToCourierResult` now carries `orderNumber` — the courier path already
      loads the order (`loadOrderForCourier` selects `number`), so nothing extra
      is fetched. Both actions revalidate the number path plus the invoice path,
      matching what `order-actions.ts` does; the invoice renders `trackingCode`
      too, so it had the same staleness.

- [x] AD-341 R1 + R3 closed — the last two staff-facing order-ID gaps.

      **R1 — the new-order alert linked by cuid.** The title already read

      alert disagreed with itself. Now links by number.

      The href is PERSISTED on the `StaffAlert` row, which makes this different
